(5.21)

Formally (
i.e.
by comparing the leading expansion terms), we can generalise these relations to products of the infinite series
\mathcal{I}_{k_{1}}\,\mathcal{I}_{k_{2}}
(for integers
k_{1,2}\geq 0
), as defined in eq. (
A.21
), which can be re-expanded in a finite sum of
\mathcal{I}_{k}
with coefficients that are functions of Eisenstein series. Based on the leading examples in
k_{1,2}
, we have found the following empiric relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{k_{1}}(u,\rho)\,\mathcal{I}_{k_{2}}(u,\rho)=-\frac{B_{2\bar{k}}}{2\bar{k}}\,\mathfrak{d}E_{2\bar{k}}(\rho)-\sum_{k=0}^{\bar{k}}\frac{B_{2\bar{k}-2k}E_{2\bar{k}-2k}\mathcal{P}_{k}(k_{1},k_{2})}{(2k+1)!}\,\mathcal{I}_{k}(u,\rho)\,,

for

\displaystyle\bar{k}=k_{1}+k_{2}+1\,,

(5.22)

which we have checked up to
k_{1}=k_{2}=6
. Here the derivatives
\mathfrak{d}E_{2n}
of the Eisenstein series have been defined in (
A.27
) and
E_{0}=1
is understood. Furthermore,
\mathcal{P}_{k}(k_{1},k_{2})
are symmetric polynomials of order
2(k+1)
with integer coefficients, for which more information is provided in Appendix
C
. In writing eq. (
5.22
) we have implicitly assumed that the sums
\mathcal{I}_{k}
are convergent. We leave a more systematic (and rigorous) study of the relations (
5.22
) to future work, but simply remark that up to the level we have checked (
i.e.
up to
k_{1}+k_{2}=10
) they are sufficient to relate the results found here to the decompositions of the free energy provided in
[
44
]
(and to fix any ambiguities pointed out there).

6
Conclusions and Outlook

In this paper we have analysed the topological string partition function on a class of toric, non-compact Calabi-Yau threefolds
X_{N,1}
. Physically, these capture (among others) the instanton partition function of Little String Theories, whose low energy limit are gauge theories with gauge group
U(N)
and matter in the adjoint representation. We have shown for generic deformation parameters
\epsilon_{1,2}
that for fixed instanton level, the contribution to the partition function that depends on the gauge structure can be written in terms of simple elliptic modular graph functions: indeed, we have identified elementary building blocks
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha_{j}\alpha_{i}}
(see eq. (
3.3
)) that are labelled by two integer partitions and which can be re-written in terms of Kronecker-Eisenstein series as in eq. (
3.6
). In the unrefined limit (
i.e.
for
\epsilon_{1}=-\epsilon_{2}=\epsilon
), the latter re-arrange themselves in terms of differences of propagators of a free chiral boson on the torus. We have shown that this re-organisation of the partition function is responsible for the Feynman-diagrammatic decomposition of the non-perturbative free energy observed previously in the literature
[
43
,
44
]
. Moreover, we have provided an interpretation of the origin of the propagator factors both geometrically (in terms of counting holomorphic curves on the geometry
X_{N,1}
) and physically (in terms of Nekrasov subfunctions of the
U(N)
gauge theory).

In a different limit of the deformation parameters (
i.e.
the leading singularity in
\epsilon_{2}
, which we have called the NS-limit), we have shown for
N=1
a recursive structure, which allows to express higher instanton contributions as powers of the leading one. We have provided strong evidence that this structure also persists for higher
N
. Including corrections (in terms of
\epsilon_{2}
) to the NS limit, we have shown that a similar hierarchy structure exists in principle for the entire partition function, however, non-trivial coefficient functions appear. Implicitly, these relations govern the precise numerical factors that appear in Feynman diagrammatic expansion of the free energy.

The results of this work strengthen the idea that the non-perturbative partition function (and thus also the free energy) of a class of LSTs (whose low energy limit are supersymmetric gauge theories with gauge group
U(N)
) allows a decomposition in terms of propagators of free two-dimensional scalar fields. We have found a precise relation between Nekrasov-subfunctions of the low energy theory and combinations of Greens functions of free scalar fields on the torus. At the same time, AGT-like correspondences
[
69
]
for (compactifications of) LSTs have been studied
[
50
]
, establishing a connection to
q
-deformed Toda (and Liouville theories). In the future it will be important to understand the deeper physical connection to the results presented in this work. Moreover, it would be interesting to see if a possible connection to eMGF that we pointed out can cast further light on the computation of conformal blocks in the two-dimensional theory. Similarly, it would be interesting to understand how the results presented here are related to the reformulation of the non-perturbative partition function of a
\Gamma
-quiver gauge theory in terms of a correlator in an underlying
W(\Gamma)
-algebra that was found in
[
87
]
.

Furthermore, it will be interesting in the future to extend our analysis to more general models of LSTs (see
e.g.

[
88
]
for a classification of such theories using F-theory compactifications). Indeed, as a simple generalisation, models of M5-branes probing non-trivial
\mathbb{Z}_{M}
orbifold geometries have been studied for which the partition function can be computed using the same technology that we have used here
[
37
,
10
,
12
,
11
]
. It would be interesting to extend our results to this class of models. Moreover, it would be interesting to see if similar decompositions as the ones discussed here also exist in LSTs that are not of
A_{N}
-type.

Appendix A
Notation and Conventions

A.1
Non-Perturbative Topological String Partition Function

In this appendix we collect our conventions and notation used to write the partition function discussed in Section
2
. Starting from the parameters
(h_{1},\ldots,h_{N},v_{1},\ldots,v_{N},m_{1},\ldots,m_{N})
introduced in Figure
1
, we first define

\displaystyle Q_{h_{k}}=e^{2\pi ih_{k}}\,,

\displaystyle Q_{v_{k}}=e^{2\pi iv_{k}}\,,

\displaystyle Q_{m_{k}}=e^{2\pi im_{k}}\,,

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{k}=Q_{h_{k}}Q_{v_{k}}\,,

\displaystyle\forall k=1,\ldots,N\,.

(A.1)

Throughout this appendix, we consider the indices
i,j,k=1,\ldots,N
to be defined modulo
N
(
e.g.

Q_{h_{N+1}}=Q_{h_{1}}
). With this convention, we furthermore define
[
11
]

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{i,j}=Q_{h_{i}}\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}\widetilde{Q}_{i-k}\,,

\displaystyle\dot{Q}_{i,j}=\widetilde{Q}_{i+1}\ldots\widetilde{Q}_{i+j}\,,

\displaystyle\overline{Q}_{i,j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}1&\text{if}&j=N\,,\\
Q_{h_{i}}Q_{v_{i-j}}\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}\widetilde{Q}_{i-k}&\text{if}&j\neq L\,.\end{array}\right.

For the basis of Kähler parameters
(\tau,S,\rho,\widehat{a}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{a}_{N-1})
defined in (
2.2
), we introduce

\displaystyle Q_{\tau}=e^{2\pi i\tau}\,,

\displaystyle Q_{S}=e^{2\pi iS}\,,

\displaystyle Q_{\rho}=e^{2\pi i\rho}\,,

\displaystyle Q_{\widehat{a}_{i}}=e^{2\pi i\widehat{a}_{i}}\,\hskip 28.45274pt\forall i=1,\ldots,N\,.

(A.4)

and for the deformation parameters
\epsilon_{1,2}
of the refined topological vertex
[
33
,
34
]

\displaystyle q=e^{2\pi i\epsilon_{1}}\,,

and

\displaystyle t=e^{-2\pi i\epsilon_{2}}\,.

(A.5)

In order to write the partition function (
2.3
) we next need to review a number of definitions
[
15
,
11
]
. For
\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{\ell(\mu)})
an integer partition of length
\ell(\mu)
and
\mu^{t}
its transpose, let

\displaystyle|\mu|=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell(\mu)}\mu_{i}\,,

\displaystyle||\mu||^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell(\mu)}\mu_{i}^{2}\,,

\displaystyle||\mu^{t}||^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell(\mu^{t})}(\mu_{i}^{t})^{2}\,.

(A.6)

We then define

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mu\nu}(x;q,t)

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}J_{\mu\nu}(Q_{\rho}^{k-1}x;q,t)\,,

\displaystyle J_{\mu\nu}(x;q,t)

\displaystyle=\prod_{(i,j)\in\mu}\left(1-xq^{\nu_{j}^{t}-i+\frac{1}{2}}t^{\mu_{i}-j+\frac{1}{2}}\right)\prod_{(i,j)\in\nu}\left(1-xq^{-\mu_{j}^{t}+i-\frac{1}{2}}t^{-\nu_{i}+j-\frac{1}{2}}\right)\,,

(A.7)

where
(i,j)\in\mu
denote the positions of the boxes in the Young diagram associated with the partition
\mu
. Products of
\mathcal{J}_{\mu\nu}
can be simplified using the relations

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mu\nu}(x;q,t)\,\mathcal{J}_{\nu\mu}(Q_{\rho}x^{-1};q,t)

\displaystyle=x^{\frac{|\mu|+|\nu|}{2}}\,q^{\frac{||\nu^{t}||^{2}-||\mu^{t}||^{2}}{4}}t^{\frac{||\mu||^{2}-||\nu||^{2}}{4}}\,\vartheta_{\mu\nu}(x;\rho)\,,

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{|\mu|}t^{\frac{||\mu||^{2}}{2}}q^{\frac{||\mu^{t}||^{2}}{2}}\widetilde{Z}_{\mu}(q,t)\widetilde{Z}_{\mu^{t}}(t,q)}{\mathcal{J}_{\mu\mu}(Q_{\rho}\sqrt{t/q};q,t)\mathcal{J}_{\mu\mu}(Q_{\rho}\sqrt{q/t};q,t)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\vartheta_{\mu\mu}(\sqrt{q/t};\rho)}=\frac{1}{\vartheta_{\mu\mu}(\sqrt{t/q};\rho)}\,,

(A.8)

where we have defined the theta-functions (with
x=e^{2\pi iz}
)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\mu\nu}(x;\rho)

\displaystyle=\prod_{(i,j)\in\mu}\vartheta\left(x^{-1}q^{-\nu_{j}^{t}+i-\frac{1}{2}}t^{-\mu_{i}+j-\frac{1}{2}};\rho\right)\prod_{(i,j)\in\nu}\vartheta\left(x^{-1}q^{\mu_{j}^{t}-i+\frac{1}{2}}t^{\nu_{i}-j+\frac{1}{2}};\rho\right)\,,

\displaystyle\vartheta(x;\rho)

\displaystyle=\left(x^{1/2}-x^{-1/2}\right)\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}(1-xQ_{\rho}^{k})(1-x^{-1}Q_{\rho}^{k})=\frac{iQ_{\rho}^{-1/8}\theta_{1}(z;\rho)}{\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}(1-Q_{\rho}^{k})}\,,

(A.9)

with
\theta_{1}
the Jacobi-theta function. The factors
\widetilde{Z}_{\mu}
in (
A.8
) appear naturally in the partition function (
2.3
) as part of
\widehat{Z}
, namely

\displaystyle\widehat{Z}=\prod_{k=1}^{N}t^{\frac{||\alpha_{k}||^{2}}{2}}q^{\frac{||\alpha_{k}^{t}||^{2}}{2}}\,\widetilde{Z}_{\alpha_{k}}(q,t)\,\widetilde{Z}_{\alpha^{t}_{k}}(t,q)\,,

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{Z}_{\alpha}(q,t)=\prod_{(i,j)\in\alpha}\left(1-q^{\nu_{j}^{t}-i+1}t^{\nu_{i}-j}\right)^{-1}\,.

(A.10)

Finally, the normalisation factor
W_{N}(\emptyset)
in (
2.3
) has an interpretation as a closed topological string amplitude and the precise definition (which shall not be needed in this paper) can be found in
[
11
]
.

A.2
Modular Objects

We define a weak Jacobi form of index
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and weight
w\in\mathbb{N}
as a holomorphic function
\phi:\,\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{H}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
which transforms in the following manner

\displaystyle\phi\left(\frac{z}{c\rho+d},\frac{a\rho+b}{c\rho+d}\right)=(c\rho+d)^{w}\,e^{\frac{2\pi imcz^{2}}{c\rho+d}}\,\phi(z,\rho)\,,

\displaystyle\forall\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})\,,

\displaystyle\phi(z+k_{1}\rho+k_{2},\rho)=e^{-2\pi im(k_{1}^{2}\rho+2k_{1}z)}\phi(z,\rho)\,,

\displaystyle\forall k_{1,2}\in\mathbb{N}\,,

(A.13)

and which has the following Fourier expansion

\displaystyle\phi(z,\rho)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}c(n,k)\,Q_{\rho}^{n}\,e^{2\pi izk}\,,

with

\displaystyle c(n,k)=(-1)^{w}\,c(n,-k)\,.

(A.14)

We define the following standard Jacobi forms of index
m=1
and weights
w=-2
and
w=0

\displaystyle\phi_{-2}(z;\rho)=\frac{\theta_{1}^{2}(z;\rho)}{\eta^{6}(\rho)}\,,

and

\displaystyle\phi_{0}(z;\rho)=8\sum_{i=2}^{4}\left(\frac{\theta_{i}(z;\rho)}{\theta_{i}(0;\rho)}\right)^{2}\,,

(A.15)

where
\theta_{i=1,2,3,4}
are the Jacobi theta functions, notably

\displaystyle\theta_{1}(z;\rho)=-iQ_{\rho}^{1/8}e^{\pi iz}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(1-Q_{\rho}^{n}\right)\left(1-e^{2\pi iz}\,Q_{\rho}^{n}\right)\left(1-e^{-2\pi iz}\,Q_{\rho}^{n-1}\right)\,,

(A.16)

and
\eta
the Dedekind eta function

\displaystyle\eta(\rho)=Q_{\rho}^{1/24}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-Q_{\rho}^{n})\,.

(A.17)

We note the followig shift symmetry of
\theta_{1}

\displaystyle\theta_{1}(z\pm\rho;\rho)=-\,Q_{\rho}^{-1/2}\,e^{\mp 2\pi iz}\,\theta_{1}(z;\rho)\,.

(A.18)

Furthermore, we define the Eisenstein series

\displaystyle E_{2n}(\rho)=1-\frac{4n}{B_{2n}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{2n-1}(k)\,Q_{\rho}^{k}\,,

with

\displaystyle n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\,,

(A.19)

with
B_{2n}
the Bernoulli numbers and
\sigma_{n}(k)
the divisor sigma function. The
E_{2n}
are holomorphic modular forms of weight
2n
, while
E_{2}
can be completed into a quasi-modular form of weight
2

\displaystyle\widehat{E}_{2}(\rho,\bar{\rho})=E_{2}(\rho)-\frac{3}{\pi\,\text{Im}(\rho)}\,.

(A.20)

We also define
[
43
,
44
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{k}(\widehat{a},\rho)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{n^{2k+1}}{1-Q_{\rho}^{n}}\left(Q_{\widehat{a}}^{n}+\frac{Q_{\rho}^{n}}{Q_{\widehat{a}}^{n}}\right)\,,

(A.21)

which can be computed iteratively

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{k}(\widehat{a},\rho)=D_{\widehat{a}}^{2k}\,\mathcal{I}_{0}(\widehat{a},\rho)\,,

with

\displaystyle D_{\widehat{a}}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\widehat{a}}=Q_{\widehat{a}}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial Q_{\widehat{a}}}\,,

(A.22)

where
\mathcal{I}_{0}
can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{0}(\widehat{a},\rho)=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\left[2\zeta(2)\,E_{2}(\rho)+\wp(\widehat{a};\rho)\right]=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\left[\mathbb{G}^{\prime\prime}(\widehat{a};\rho)+\frac{2\pi i}{\rho-\bar{\rho}}\right]\,.

(A.23)

Here
\wp
is Weierstrass’ elliptic function, which is defined as

\displaystyle\wp(z;\rho)=\frac{1}{z^{2}}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2(2k+1)\zeta(2k+2)\,E_{2k+2}(\rho)\,z^{2k}\,,

(A.24)

and which can expressed as
[
89
,
90
]

\displaystyle\wp(z;\rho)=\zeta(2)\,\frac{\phi_{0}(z;\rho)}{\phi_{-2}(z;\rho)}\,.

(A.25)

\mathbb{G}
appearing in (
A.23
) is the two-point function of a free scalar field on the torus (and a prime denotes the derivative with respect to
z
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}(z;\rho)=-\ln\left|\frac{\theta_{1}(z;\rho)}{\theta^{\prime}_{1}(0;\rho)}\right|^{2}-\frac{\pi}{2\text{Im}(\rho)}\,(z-\bar{z})^{2}\,.

(A.26)

For use in Section
5.3
, we introduce the following derivatives of the Eisenstein series

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}E_{2n}(\rho)=Q_{\rho}\,\frac{dE_{2n}(\rho)}{dQ_{\rho}}\,,

(A.27)

which are quasi-modular forms of weight
2n+2
that can be written as homogeneous polynomials in
(E_{2},E_{4},E_{6})
,
e.g.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}E_{2}=\frac{1}{12}(E_{2}^{2}-E_{4})\,,\hskip 28.45274pt\mathfrak{d}E_{4}=\frac{1}{3}(E_{2}E_{4}-E_{6})\,,\hskip 28.45274pt\mathfrak{d}E_{6}=\frac{1}{2}(E_{2}E_{6}-E_{4}^{2})\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}E_{8}=\frac{2}{3}\,E_{4}\,(E_{2}E_{4}-E_{6})\,,\hskip 28.45274pt\mathfrak{d}E_{10}=\frac{1}{6}(5E_{2}E_{4}E_{6}-3E_{4}^{3}-2E_{6}^{2})\,.

(A.28)

These derivatives can be used to define meromorphic Jacobi forms of index
0
and weight
2n
. Indeed, we have verified up to
n=6
that in an expansion in powers of
\widehat{a}
all
E_{2}
cancel out in the following combinations

\displaystyle 2nE_{2n}(\rho)\,\mathcal{I}_{0}(\widehat{a},\rho)+\mathfrak{d}E_{2n}(\rho)\,,

\displaystyle\forall n>1\,.

(A.29)

Finally, we also define the
Hecke operator
: let
J_{w,m}
be the space of Jacobi forms of index
m
and weight
w
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{k}:\,\,J_{w,m}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow J_{w,km}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle\phi(z,\rho)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{H}_{k}(\phi(z,\rho))=k^{w-1}\sum_{{d|k}\atop{b\text{ mod }d}}d^{-w}\,\phi\left(\frac{kz}{d},\frac{k\rho+bd}{d^{2}}\right)\,.

(A.30)

A.3
Elliptic Modular Graph Forms

In this appendix we review important basic notation and definitions related to elliptic modular graph functions and forms (eMFG). Our conventions follow mainly
[
51
,
52
,
91
]
.

Modular graph functions

[
45
,
46
,
80
,
81
,
83
,
84
,
85
,
93
,
94
,
92
]
are maps from graphs of
N
vertices connected by (decorated and oriented) edges, to the space of modular invariant functions. They can be generalised to
modular graph forms
which are maps from such graphs into the space of (non-holomorphic) modular forms. Such maps are important in the study of scattering amplitudes in string theory and (supersymmetric) gauge theories (see
e.g.

[
95
,
96
]
) and have been systematically studied in recent years (see
e.g.

[
97
,
98
,
99
]
). The concept of modular graph functions and forms can further be generalise to
elliptic modular graph
(functions)
forms
(eMFG)
[
51
,
52
]
as maps from graphs to (non-holomorphic) elliptic functions on the torus that are (invariant) covariant under modular transformations. A simple example is the function
\mathbb{G}
defined in (
A.26
), which is the scalar Greens-function (
i.e.
the 2-point function) of a free scalar on the torus. Let

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle=\{z\in\mathbb{C}|z=m\,\rho+n\text{ for }(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\}\,,

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\{z\in\mathbb{C}|z=m\,\rho+n\text{ for }(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\text{ and }(m,n)\neq(0,0)\}\,,

(A.31)

then
\mathbb{G}(z;\rho)
can also be written in the following form

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}(z;\rho)=\frac{\rho-\bar{\rho}}{2\pi i}\sum_{p\in\Lambda^{\prime}}\frac{\chi_{p}(z;\rho)}{|p|^{2}}\,.

(A.32)

Here we have introduced

\displaystyle\chi_{p}(z;\rho)=e^{2\pi i(nr-ms)}

with

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}z=r\,\rho+s\hskip 14.22636pt\text{ for }r,s\in[0,1]\\
p=m\,\rho+n\in\Lambda^{\prime}\,,\end{array}

which satisfy the relations

\displaystyle\chi_{p_{1}+p_{2}}(z;\rho)=\chi_{p_{1}}(z;\rho)\,\chi_{p_{2}}(z;\rho)\,,

\displaystyle\chi_{p}(z_{1}+z_{2};\rho)=\chi_{p}(z_{1};\rho)\,\chi_{p}(z_{2};\rho)\,,

\displaystyle\chi_{-p}(z;\rho)=\chi_{p}(-z,\rho)\,.

A building block for more sophisticated eMGF is the
Kronecker-Eisenstein series

[
100
,
101
,
102
]

\displaystyle\Omega(u,v;\rho)=\sum_{p\in\Lambda}\frac{\chi_{p}(u;\rho)}{v-p}=\frac{1}{v}+\sum_{p\in\Lambda^{\prime}}\frac{\chi_{p}(u;\rho)}{v-p}\,,

\displaystyle\forall u,v\in\mathbb{C}\,.

(A.35)

As a function of
u
,
\Omega
is invariant under
u\to u+\kappa
for any
\kappa\in\Lambda
and as a function of
v
,
\Omega
is meromorphic with simple poles for
v\in\Lambda
. It can also be written in terms of Jacobi-theta functions (
A.16
), which make these properties more tangible

\displaystyle\Omega(u,v;\rho)=\text{exp}\left(2\pi iv\frac{{\rm Im}(u)}{{\rm Im}(\rho)}\right)\frac{\theta_{1}(u+v;\rho)\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0;\rho)}{\theta_{1}(u;\rho)\theta_{1}(v;\rho)}\,,

\displaystyle\forall u,v\in\mathbb{C}\,,

(A.36)

where
\theta^{\prime}_{1}(0;\rho)=2\pi\eta^{3}(\rho)
, with
\eta
the Dedekind eta function defined in (
A.17
).

We also remark the following important relation between a product of two
\Omega
with related arguments and the difference of two Weierstrass elliptic functions introduced in (
A.24
)

\displaystyle\Omega(u,v;\rho)\,\Omega(u,-v;\rho)=-4\pi^{2}\eta^{6}(\rho)\,\frac{\theta_{1}(u+v;\rho)\theta_{1}(u-v;\rho)}{\theta_{1}^{2}(u;\rho)\,\theta_{1}^{2}(v;\rho)}=\wp(u)-\wp(v)\,,

\displaystyle\forall u,v\in\mathbb{C}\,.

(A.37)

Using (see eq. (
A.23
))
\wp(u)=\mathbb{G}^{\prime\prime}(u;\rho)-2\zeta(2)\,\widehat{E}_{2}(\rho)
, we can therefore write the combination (
A.37
) of Kronecker-Eisenstein series in terms of the difference of two scalar-Greens functions on the torus

\displaystyle\Omega(u,v;\rho)\,\Omega(u,-v;\rho)=\mathbb{G}^{\prime\prime}(u;\rho)-\mathbb{G}^{\prime\prime}(v;\rho)\,.

(A.38)

Appendix B
Propagator Building Blocks

In this Appendix we review some of the objects introduced in
[
44
]
. Our notation is slightly adapted to the current paper.

B.1
External States

In order to expand the free energy in a form that resembles Feynman diagrams, in
[
44
]
certain ’external states’ have been defined. In this work we shall use a more condensed notation for the leading contributions in
\epsilon_{1,2}
:

•

free energy of the configuration

The non-perturbative free energy associated with the web diagram
(1,1)
was expanded in
[
44
]
for
q=t=e^{2\pi i\epsilon}
in the following form

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1,1}(\rho,S,\tau;\epsilon)=\ln\mathcal{Z}_{1,1}(\rho,S,\tau;\epsilon)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}Q_{\tau}^{n}\,\epsilon^{2s-2}\,H^{{(n),\{0\}}}_{(s)}(S,\rho)\,.

(B.1)

In this work we shall use the following condensed notation for the coefficients with
s=0
, namely
H_{r}(S,\rho):=H^{{(1),\{0\}}}_{(s)}(S,\rho)
which are weak Jacobi forms of weight
-2
and index
r
. They are related through a Hecke transformation (see
[
44
]
)

\displaystyle H_{r}(S,\rho)=\mathcal{H}_{r}\left(H_{1}(S,\rho)\right)\,,

(B.2)

where the Hecke operator is defined in (
A.30
). The first few instances are given by

\displaystyle H_{1}=-\phi_{-2}\,,\hskip 25.6073ptH_{2}=-\frac{1}{16}\,\phi_{0}\,\phi_{-2}\,,\hskip 25.6073ptH_{3}=-\frac{\phi_{-2}}{432}\,\left(\phi_{0}^{2}+12\,E_{4}\,\phi_{-2}^{2}\right)\,.

(B.3)

•

W
function

In
[
9
,
38
]
the function
W^{{(1)}}_{(0)}
was found to be related to counting BPS states of a single M5-brane with M2-branes ending on both sides of it

\displaystyle W(S,\rho;\epsilon)=\frac{\theta_{1}^{2}(S;\rho)-\theta_{1}(S+\epsilon;\rho)\,\theta_{1}(S-\epsilon;\rho)}{\theta_{1}^{2}(\epsilon;\rho)}=\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\epsilon^{2s}\,W^{{(1)}}_{(s)}(S,\rho)\,.

(B.4)

For the coefficients
s=0
we shall use the shorthand notation
W_{r}(S,\rho)=W^{{(r)}}_{(0)}(S,\rho)
in this article, where the
W_{r}
are quasi-Jacobi forms of weight
0
and index
r
. Similar to the
H_{r}
, they can (formally) also be related through Hecke transformations

\displaystyle W_{r}(S,\rho)=\mathcal{H}_{r}\left(W_{1}(S,\rho)\right)\,,

(B.5)

with
\mathcal{H}_{r}
defined in (
A.30
). The first few instances of
W_{r}
are given explicitly by

\displaystyle W_{1}=\frac{1}{24}\,(\phi_{0}+2\,E_{2}\,\phi_{-2})\,,\hskip 28.45274ptW_{2}=\frac{1}{384}\,\left(\phi_{0}^{2}+4\,E_{2}\,\phi_{0}\,\phi_{-2}+4\,E_{4}\,\phi_{-2}^{2}\right)\,,

(B.6)

\displaystyle W_{3}=\frac{1}{10368}\left(\phi_{0}^{3}+6E_{2}\phi_{0}^{2}\phi_{-2}+12E_{4}\phi_{0}\phi_{-2}^{2}+8(9E_{2}E_{4}-8E_{6})\right)\,.

(B.7)

B.2
Two Points

In addition to the definition (
5.8
), we also introduce a graphical notation for the derivatives of the scalar two-point function (in the sense of (
A.22
)) by inserting
2k
-crosses

\displaystyle\forall k>1\,.

(B.8)

While the two-point function

in (
5.8
) (which is equal to (
A.23
)) is a quasi-Jacobi form, the quantities with derivatives in eq. (
B.8
) are meromorphic Jacobi forms since only the term
\mathcal{O}(\widehat{a}^{0})
in (
A.23
) contains
E_{2}
.

For vanishing
\epsilon
, the two-point function (
5.8
) can furthermore be related to (
5.3
) in the following manner

We notice that this two-point function is manifestly modular and holomorphic, however, (compared to (
5.8
)) it is also a function of
S
.

}\hskip 7.11317pt=\hskip 7.11317pt\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{4}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\sum_{\mathcal{S}\in\{1,2,3\}\setminus\{\ell\}\atop{|\mathcal{S}|=2}}\prod_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathcal{S}}\mathbb{G}^{(4)}(\widehat{b}_{\ell}-\widehat{b}_{j_{1}};\rho)\,\left(\mathbb{G}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(\widehat{b}_{\ell}-\widehat{b}_{j_{2}};\rho)+\frac{2\pi i}{\rho-\bar{\rho}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle\parbox{79.6678pt}{\hbox to80.55pt{\vbox to87.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 59.4812pt\lower-18.3639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}\hskip 7.11317pt=\hskip 7.11317pt\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{4}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\prod_{j\neq\ell}\mathbb{G}^{(4)}(\widehat{b}_{\ell}-\widehat{b}_{j};\rho)\,,

where the symbol

\sum

indicates that a cyclic sum over all points has been taken into account.

Appendix C
Propagator Polynomials

In this appendix, we provide the leading examples of the polynomials
\mathcal{P}_{k}(k_{1},k_{2})
appearing in eq. (
5.22
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{0}

\displaystyle=1\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{1}

\displaystyle=4(k_{1}^{2}-k_{1}k_{2}+k_{2}^{2})-1\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{2}

\displaystyle=16(k_{1}^{4}-k_{1}^{3}k_{2}+k_{2}^{2}k_{2}^{2}-k_{1}k_{2}^{3}+k_{2}^{4})-8(3k_{1}^{3}-k_{1}^{2}k_{2}-k_{1}k_{2}^{2}+3k_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07182pt-4(k_{1}^{2}-3k_{1}k_{2}+k_{2}^{2})+6(k_{1}+k_{2})\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{3}

\displaystyle=64(k_{1}^{6}-k_{1}^{5}k_{2}+k_{1}^{4}k_{2}-k_{1}^{3}k_{2}^{3}+k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{4}-k_{1}k_{2}^{5}+k_{2}^{6})

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07182pt-64(5k_{1}^{5}-3k_{1}^{4}k_{2}+k_{1}^{3}k_{2}^{2}+k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{3}-3k_{1}k_{2}^{4}+5k_{2}^{5})+32(15k_{1}^{4}-3k_{1}^{2}k_{2}-k_{1}^{2}k_{2}^{2}-3k_{1}k_{2}^{3}+15k_{2}^{4})

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07182pt-32(5k_{1}^{3}+k_{1}^{2}k_{2}+k_{1}k_{2}^{2}+5k_{2}^{3})-4(31k_{1}^{2}-15k_{1}k_{2}+31k_{2}^{2})+60(k_{1}+k_{2})\,.

(C.1)

We remark that these can also be characterised in the following way (which lends itself to generalisation to higher
k
):

\displaystyle f_{k}(x)=\frac{x^{2k+1}}{1-Q_{\rho}^{x}}\,,

for

\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\,,

(C.2)

then one can decompose the convolution of two such functions in the following way

\displaystyle(f_{k_{1}}\star f_{k_{2}})(x):

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}dy\,f_{k_{1}}(y)\,f_{k_{2}}(x-y)=\frac{1}{1-Q_{\rho}^{x}}\sum_{k=0}^{k_{1}+k_{2}+1}c_{k_{1}k_{2}k}\,\frac{x^{2k+1}}{\rho^{2(k_{1}+k_{2}-k+1)}}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{k=0}^{k_{1}+k_{2}+1}\,\frac{B_{2(k_{1}+k_{2}-k+1)}\,\mathcal{P}_{k}(k_{1},k_{2})}{(2k+1)!\,\rho^{2(k_{1}+k_{2}-k+1)}}\,f_{k}(x)\,.

(C.3)

We have furthermore observed a few further properties of the polynomials
\mathcal{P}_{k}(k_{1},k_{2})
. Indeed, the highest powers in each case follow the pattern
2^{2k}\sum_{a=0}^{2k}(-1)^{a}k_{1}^{a}k_{2}^{2k-a}
, while subleading contributions are characterised by particular symmetries. We have notably observed

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{k}(k-k_{1}-1,k-k_{2}-1)=\mathcal{P}_{k}(k_{1},k_{2})\,,

(C.4)

and we have verified up to
k=10
that they factorise for certain values of their arguments

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{k}(k_{1},-\tfrac{1}{2})=\mathcal{P}_{k}(k_{1},k_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2})=\prod_{j=0}^{2k-1}(2k_{1}+1-j)\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{k}(k_{1},k_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2})=\mathcal{P}_{k}(k_{1},k-\tfrac{1}{2})=\prod_{j=1}^{2k}(2k_{1}+1-j)\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{k}(k_{1},k_{1})=\mathcal{P}_{k}(k_{1},\tfrac{k-1}{2})=\frac{\prod_{j=0}^{2k}(2k_{1}+1-j)}{2k_{1}+1-k}\,.

(C.5)

Finally, up to
k=10
the
\mathcal{P}_{k}
vanish for specific values of their arguments

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{k}(0,j)=0\,,

\displaystyle\forall j=1,\ldots,k-2\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{k}(\tfrac{j-1}{2},\tfrac{\ell-1}{2})=0\,,

\displaystyle\forall\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\ell=1,\ldots,2k-2-j\,,\\
j=1,\ldots,2k\,.\end{array}\right.

We have also verified that the properties mentioned in this appendix uniquely fix the polynomial
\mathcal{P}_{k}
up to
k=10
.
